[trialAccount t18270913-84]
1672. WILLIAM BATTEY was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of July, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of George Pocock, from his person.
GEORGE POCOCK. I was going across Clements'-inn on the 27th of July, a little before ten o'clock in the evening; I felt something at my pocket - I turned and saw the prisoner dropping this handkerchief from him - it was about half way between his hand and the ground when I saw it. I took hold of him, and had a great difficulty in taking him - in going down Pickett-street, I met a person, who tried to assist me - the prisoner struck at him, and he retreated; the prisoner then struck me violently, and made my nose bleed; he got from me for a moment, but was taken at the coach-stand, and the watchman came up.
WILLIAM NESBITT. I am a constable. I assisted in taking the prisoner from a tobacconist's-shop, where the prosecutor had taken him - he struggled very much.
Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor turned round, and said I had taken his handkerchief. I said I was not there - he then struck me with his umbrella, and he first said there was a mark on the handkerchief, but there was not - there was a boy five or six yards from me - he passed him to come to me - he took the handkerchief off the ground - I had not had it.
MR. POCOCK. I thought at first there was a mark on it, but it is washed out; I have not the least doubt of its being mine.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18270913-85]
1673. JOHN PICKARD was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of July, 1 reticule, value 2l.; 2 purses, value 30s.; 1 handkerchief, value 1s.; 1 bunch of keys, value 1s.; 3 sovereigns and 2 sixpences, the property of Robert Harwood, from the person of Mary, his wife.
MARY HARWOOD. I am the wife of Robert Harwood. On the 16th of July I was crossing the fields from Hackney, and two men passed me and my little boy - one of them had a basket in his hand, and my little boy drew my attention to it, as being a nice basket to hold froit - I was afterwards going through a stile; the prisoner and the other man came up to me; the other man snatched my reticule, fore it off my arm, and tore my dress in doing it - the prisoner did not touch me; he had hold of the other man's arm - I believe they were both equally close to me- I had just parted with my little boy; the prisoner and the other ran off across the fields - I have not seen the property since.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Have you no other name than Mary? A. No; I am the wife of Captain Robert Harwood; I am sure the prisoner was with the man who took it - I do not know that he parted with it to the prisoner - the other man got over a bank and escaped, but the prisoner was taken - they had passed me before, and the prisoner looked at me in going along.
OCTAVIUS HARWOOD. I am the prosecutrix's son. I had just left my mother, when the reticule was taken - I had seen the prisoner and another person going across the fields, about ten minutes before the reticule was snatched; I am certain the prisoner was one of the men.
Cross-examined. Q. Were not they before you? A. They passed us and went into the road to Clapton - I saw their faces as they passed within about two yards of us- I saw their side faces when I left my mother - I saw them again coming towards the stile where she was - they passed by me very quiet - the other man had a different face to this man; but I cannot describe his face.
COURT. Q. Were you before the Magistrate the next day? A. Yes; I saw the prisoner there then; I had no doubt of his being one of them.
WILLIAM GALLANT. I am a cow-keeper, and live at Clapton. On the 16th of July, I heard the cry of Stop thief! in the fields - I went a few yards, and saw the prisoner and another coming towards me - I asked them what was the matter; one of them said, "We have only been having a bit of fun with the lady;" the prisoner made to go past me, but I collared him; the other jumped over the bank and got away - there was no other person near; the lady and the young gentleman had been pursuing them, and crying Stop thief! for I suppose, fifty yards.
Cross-examined. Q. It might be the other one who said, "I have been having a bit of fun with the lady?" A. No; it was we have been having some fun with the lady; I did not see the reticule; the lady did say at first, that she thought the prisoner was one of the men, or something to that purpose.
COURT. Q. Where did she say so? A. When I had hold of the man's collar, she was quite in a flurry, but when she recovered herself, she said he was with the thief.
JOHN ELSON. I am a headborough. I took the prisoner into custody.
Prisoner's Defence. Being rather unwell I left home to take a walk - on my way home I went across this field, as the prosecutrix has stated - I stopped to look at the corn; a young man came up, and we were discoursing, I dare say for two or three minutes; this lady came by - as I was turning down a lane, I heard the cry of Stop thief! and I saw the young man who had been speaking to me rush by me - I put out my hand to stop him, but he got by - I told the witness who came up to stop him, but he let him go, being a man nearly his own size, and took me - the lady came up, and he asked if I was the person, she said No - he said, "Shall I let him go?" she said No - I believe hewas with the other, but stop till my son comes up - the young gentleman then came up, and said he believed I was the person.
Seven witnesses gave the prisoner an excellent character.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18270913-86]
1674. TIMOTHY MAGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of July, 1 purse, value 6d.; 1 half sovereign, 2 shillings, and sixpence, the property of Patrick Mallett, from his person.
PATRICK MALLETT. On the 15th of July I was going home and met the prisoner (whom I knew before,) in Maidenhead-court; he asked me into a public-house, and said he would treat me - he took me into a house, and called for a pot of beer; he would not pay for it - he wanted me to pay, and I would not - the publican took it back again; the prisoner then said, "Have you got no money?" I said, "No, and I do not want the beer; you may pay for it;" he then put his fingers to the bottom of my waistcoat pocket, where my purse was, and took it out - I told him to give it me, that nobody might know it; he then held his fist at me, and pulled me into the passage - and while I was waiting for the watchman, he bit my fingers - the watchman came; he struck the watchman, and made away - the watchman followed and came up with him again, and struck him with his stick - he was then taken - he denied having the purse at first, but when the constable got up to take him, he produced it - the money was all right in it, and a bit of black thread was in it.
EDWARD BOOTHMAN. I was the officer of the night - the prosecutor brought the prisoner in; he at first said he had no purse, and that the money he had about him was his own - but when the prosecutor described it, he produced it - they were neither of them sober.
Prisoner's Defence. He dropped his purse twice, and I took it up for him - I did not take it out of his pocket; he has known me five or six years.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270913-87]
1675. WILLIAM COOKE was indicted for embezzlement.
JOHN SMITH. I deal with the prosecutor. On the 14th of April I paid the prisoner two sovereigns on their on account; he wrote a receipt on the bill, which I have here - I frequently paid him money on their account.
CHARLES SMITH. I am in partnership with Edward Trimnell; we are gold-lace makers - the prisoner was in our employ, and received money for us; it was his duty to bring it directly to me or my partner; he did not. On the 14th of April he did not pay us any money whatever for Mr. John Smith - I am certain we never received this amount.
Prisoner's Defence. I acknowledge my offence; it was through pride and folly that I did it - I thought I should be able to replace it.
GUILTY. Aged 20.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
There were three other indictments against the prisoner.
[trialAccount t18270913-88]
1676. THOMAS ROBERT POPPY was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of July, 34lbs. weight of paper, value 11s., and 1 book, value 2s., the goods of John Singleton Taylor, his master.
MR. JOHN SMITH conducted the prosecution.
JOSEPH HARTWELL GOWER. I am a cheesemonger, and live in Crombie-row, Battle-bridge. I know the prisoner; I have bought paper of him several times within the last two or three months - the last time might be about two months ago; I bought several bundles, and paid him 4d. per 1b. I cannot recollect what quantity - I delivered some of the same paper to the constable.
Prisoner. Q. Did you hear Mr. Taylor say, if I gave up the account of the paper, he would not prosecute me? A. Yes; I think it was before your wife - he said he would not proceed against you.
WILLIAM TAMPANY. I am a cheesemonger, and live in Tottenham-court-road. I have a book which we bought of the prisoner about six months ago. I do not know what we gave for it - we generally give 41/2d. and 5d. a pound.
DANIEL TURNER. I am a cheesemonger, and live in Brewer-street. I bought some of these papers of a person who came to my shop about three months ago. I think it was a stouter man than the prisoner.
MR. JOHN SINGLETON TAYLOR. I am a solicitor - the prisoner was in my employ. I took him into my office to write, from charitable feelings - he was recommended to me from the poor-house; I did it to serve him. I received information, and his lodgings were searched - I had heard that part of our parish records were in circulation, with pieces of cheese. I am a vestry clerk - I went to Mr. Gower's, and found this large quantity of them.
WILLIAM DEAR. I am an officer, and produce the papers which were delivered to me at Hatton-garden, by Turner, Gower, and the other witness.
MR. TAYLOR. I know these papers - part of them are my writing, and part my clerks.
Prisoner. When I was in the New prison, Mr. Taylor said, if he got his papers back he would be out of the way, and not prosecute me - he has discharged two clerks for dishonesty.
MR. TAYLOR. There was a written paper sent me to sign, agreeing not to prosecute, but I declined signing it; I said I would make no compromise - I said, perhaps, if I got all my papers back, I would not. I have discharged two clerks for dishonesty.
Prisoner's Defence, On the 5th of July I left the office at dinner-time, and two officers came to search my apartment, saying, Mr. Taylor suspected I had taken some papers - I was taken to the office, and discharged next day; the Magistrate desired him to return me some papers which were taken from my lodging; but I left them for Mr. Taylor to look over - and left a note, saying he might look them over, and take what he thought proper. I left them there several days, then went and took the rest away, and sold it for waste-paper; I heard no more for some time, when he sent word that he was determined to prosecute me, unless I gave an account of what I had taken away; this was on the 7th or 8th of August. I had applied to him for 2l. 8s., which appears to have excited some vindictive feelings against me. On the 21st of August he sent me a note, stating that he wished to see me or my wife, and on the 23d I was again taken - the paper produced is precisely the same as was delivered to me at Hatton-garden - Mr. Taylorcalled on me in the New prison, and assured me he would be out of the way; his words were, "If you will give me up that green book, I will not prosecute; but if not, I will do all I can to transport you." He has discharged some clerks, and it is hard I am to bear the blame of all that is lost - I was allowed only 15s. a week, and had a difficulty to get that. I had some papers to copy at home.
MR. TAYLOR. The papers desired by the Magistrate to be returned were not these - this is a subsequent transaction.
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18270913-89]
1677. WILLIAM LLOYD was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of July, 1 hat, value 2s.; 2 boots, value 2s., and 1 book, value 6d., the goods of Alfred Phillips his master.
ALFRED PHILLIPS, ESQ. I am a barrister. I reside in the Temple; the prisoner was in my employ for about a fortnight - I received information, paid him off, and inquired if he had restored my hat and a pair of boots, which I knew were missed; he said he had - but when he was gone I found he had not. I then examined my wardrobe, and missed several things - he was apprehended.
JAMES BAKER. I found this hat on the prisoner's head, the boots on his feet, and the book in his pocket.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I asked the prosecutor if he would have the boots mended; he said, "You may take and do what you will with them;" I thought they were given me- the old hat was laying about; I took the book to read on Sunday, and forgot to return it. On Monday, when I was discharged, I went home to fetch it; I met Mr. Phillips as I returned, and he gave me in charge.
MR. PHILLIPS. I gave him none of them. I discharged him on Tuesday. I lost several other things, and thought it right to make an example of him.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Strongly recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor and Jury.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18270913-90]
1678. JOHN DEAN was indicted for embezzlement.
HENRY BYE. I am a brewer; the prisoner was in my service, and received money for me, which he should pay me as soon as he came home; he had a book to enter the day's proceedings - here is the book; he has entered some cash as received on the 2d of June, but not 6s. as received from Black - he has never paid me that 6s.
EDWARD BLACK. I gave my servant 6s. to pay the prisoner on his master's account, the beginning of June.
ESTHER DEARNE. I am servant to Mr. Black. On the 2d of June, I gave the prisoner 6s. he gave me.
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that he believed he had paid his master every thing he had received, and if not it must arise from error.
GUILTY. Aged 53.
Transported for Fourteen Years.

1679. WILLIAM WEST was indicted for embezzlement.
MARY CLARKE. I am a widow, and keep a marine store shop, in the parish of St. Margaret, Westminster; the prisoner was in my employ, and received money for me. I sent him to Mrs. Mackley, on the 2d of July, to see some rags weighed off, and to bring me back 10l. 16s. and some few pence - he never came back.
Prisoner. Q. Was I sober when I went? A. I thought so.
SARAH MACKLEY. I paid the prisoner 10l. 16s. 4d. on the 2d of July, on account of Mrs. Clarke; it was a 5l. note, the rest in gold and silver. I thought him rather stupid, but not intoxicated.

[trialAccount t18270913-92]
1680. CHARLES POPE was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of February, 1 pair of boots, value 20s., the goods of Joseph Borsley, his master.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the prosecution.
CHARLES OGLE. I am a tailor and live in North Audley-street. I have bought boots of the prisoner, but I do not know whether he delivered them to me; here is a pair I ordered of him - I do not know whether he brought them to my house.
JOHN LACY. I am an officer. I have a pair of boots here, which Mr. Borsley cannot swear to.
Cross-examined by MR. CRESWELL. Q. When did you apprehend the prisoner? A. On the 15th of July. I do not know of his being tried here that day for stealing these very boots. I took him to Mary-le-bone office, and they ordered him to Marlborough-street, as the first case was taken there.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270913-93]
1681. ELIZA COLLINS was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of September, 1 bushel of apples, value 3s., and 1 basket, value 9d., the goods of Thomas Poole.
THOMAS GIBSON. I am servant to Thomas Poole, of Covent-garden. I was told the prisoner had taken a basket of apples from the stand - I went and found her with them; they were safe five minutes before.(Basket produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I thought I paid for them - I did not intend to steal them.
GUILTY. Aged 47.

1682. JOHN BUSKIN and ROBERT THRELKED were indicted for stealing, on the 31st of August, 1 handkerchief, value 3s., the goods of Jacob Myers, from his person.
JACOB MYERS. I was in the Strand on the 31st of August, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening, I felt some person at my pocket, turned round, and saw Whittingham holding Threlked by the collar.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. You did not see the handkerchief taken? A. No; I missed it.(Property produced and sworn to.)
WILLIAM WHITTINGHAM. I was in the Strand, and saw the prisoners in company; I could not see which of them took the handkerchief, but I saw Threlked putting it into his pocket - I rushed up, and seized him - he dropped it at his feet; Buskin ran away, but a friend who was with me followed, and took him a few yards off; Buskin said he hoped I should be lenient.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it dark? A. Yes - the lampswere lighted; I was very near them - they were so close together I could not see which took it.
THRELKED'S Defence. I picked up the handkerchief, and called to the gentleman.
THRELKED - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Six Months.
BUSKIN - NOT GUILTY.

1683. THOMAS HAWKINS was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of August, 1 bedstead, value 10s., the goods of William Kennedy.
SARAH KENNEDY. I am the wife of William Kennedy, who is a broker - we live in Little St. Andrew-street. On the 25th of August the prisoner came into the shop, and offered some razors for sale, which I would not buy; he went out, and stood about the door; my daughter called out,"Mother, have you sold a bedstead?" I said No; we ran out, and took the prisoner in Little Earl-street, with it on his shoulder; it had been tied to a nail in our passage.
MARY KENNEDY. I saw the prisoner standing about, and missed the bedstead; we found the prisoner with it.
JAMES LEDGER. I am an officer, and stopped him with the bedstead on his back.
Prisoner's Defence. A man asked me to carry it for him.
GUILTY. Aged 36.

1684. ELIZA RILEY was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of July, 1 watch, value 3l.; 1 hat, value 12s.; 1 waistcoat, value 3s., and 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of Robert Bridges.
ROBERT BRIDGES. I met the prisoner in the Hay-market, on the 31st of July, and she took me to a house in Bennett-street; we slept together, and in the morning I missed these things off the floor; she was still there - I do not know that she had left the room; my clothes were still on the ground; I asked her about it; she said if I did not hold my tongue, she would get somebody to throw me out of the window, and that I should find them when I got a light - I have not found them.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not tell your friends that you had been robbed by three men in the street? A. I did so because I did not like to tell the truth - I was sober - the door was fastened inside.
MARGARET M'DONALD. I am the wife of Thomas M'Donald, and keep this house; the prisoner had lodged with me about a fortnight.
The prisoner put in a written defence, denying the charge, and stating that the door was not fastened, and she had lost her shawl at the same time.
WILLIAM ALLENSBY. I examined the door, and there was no fastening to it.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270913-97]
1685. JAMES RYAN was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of August, 21/2lbs. weight of hair, value 7s. 6d., the goods of Caleb Welch Collins, and Richard Wells; and WILLIAM JONES was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, knowing it to have been stolen.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the prosecution.
CALEB WELCH COLLINS. I am in partnership with Richard Wells; we are horse-hair merchants, and live in Chancery-lane; Ryan was our porter. In consequence of an anonymous letter I watched him, and about a quarter past nine o'clock, on the 15th of August, I saw the prisoner Jones come in, and when he went out, I followed him, and asked what he had been to the warehouse for; he said he had got a little hair; I asked how much; he seemed confused, and said about a pound or half a pound: I took him into the counting-house, and asked him what he paid for it; he seemed confused, and said Ryan could tell what he paid for it; I called Ryan, who said 18d.: I had the hair weighed, and it was 2lbs. 10ozs., the value of which is 7s. 6d. I told Ryan he must be a very bad man to deliver so much hair at such a price: he said it was a mistake altogether; I said I could not look it over, and got an officer - I had seen nothing take place between the prisoners in the shop; Ryan entreated forgiveness, and said it was his first offence.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Then Jones went into the house? A. Yes; Riley said it was his first offence, and that he had done wrong - I do not recollect his saying it was a mistake - he had lived seventeen years with us, and bore a very good character.
THOMAS WRIGHT WELLS. I am the son of Richard Wells. I went down and asked Ryan how much he had received of Jones; he seemed confused, and said 18d.; after some time he gave it to me - he turned his back; I do not know whether he took it from his jacket pocket; I had seen Jones come in, but saw nothing pass between them - they went into the wareroom together.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you say any thing to Ryan? A. I said, "Why did not you take more than 1s. 6d.?" he said it was the first time.
JONES' Defence. I went to purchase some hair; Ryan asked how much I wanted; I said about half-a-pound; he said, "I am in a hurry," and, looking at this bundle, said, "I dare say this will be half-a-pound; get your money, and I will take it into the counting-house."
RYAN - GUILTY. Aged 28.
Confined One Year.
JONES - GUILTY. Aged 59.
Transported for Fourteen Years.

Fourth Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1686. HENRY WEST was indicted for stealing, on the 22d of August, 3 shirts, value 15s., the goods of Ephraim Birch.
EPHRAIM BIRCH. I am horse-keeper to Mr. Fagg, of Hounslow-heath; I missed my shirts from a little enclosure in the front of my house, on the 22d of August.
ANN HINES. I washed these shirts, and hung them out on a line opposite the house, about ten minutes before ten o'clock in the morning of the 22d of August; I missed them a little after twelve - I know nothing of the prisoner.
RICHARD PAINTER. I am a headborough of Stanwell. In consequence of information, I pursued the prisoner, on the 22d of August, between three and four o'clock, and found him lying on the road, about three or four miles from Bedfout; when he got up I found these shirts under him; he begged I would let him go, but did not say how he became possessed of them.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I bought these shirts of a stranger, about one week before I was taken.
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Transported for Seven Years.

1687. JOHN WILKIE and WILLIAM RICHARDS were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of June, 1 timepiece, value 2l., the goods of Thomas Knight.
SARAH KNIGHT. I am the wife of Thomas Knight, a cow-keeper, and live in Upper York-street; this timepiece was on a chest of drawers in the front kitchen; I was in the parlour on the 14th of June, about eleven o'clock in the morning, and saw a boy come up the area steps and run away with something; he was about the size of Wilkie - I ran after him - he got away into some houses in Nelson-place.
JAMES TILLING. I was in my shop, which is about thirty yards from Knight's, and saw Wilkie run by my door with something under his frock-coat, endeavouring to conceal it - my house is at a corner, leading to Nelson-place; I went to my door, and saw Mrs. Knight - I went to look for Wilkie, but he was gone; I knew him before - I believe he had a white hat on.
DIANA ROBERTS. I live in Green-street; my husband is a smith. On the 22d of June I took in a parcel from Richards' father - I did not open it; he asked me to take it for him - I gave it up to Field.
WILLIAM FIELD. This time-piece was brought in a bundle to my house - I do not know who by, as I was not at home - I took it to Knight's.
PHILIP WEBSTER. I am an officer. I received an anonymous letter from Brompton; I went to Knight's, and found they had lost a time-piece. About the 12th of July I took Wilkie, near Maida-hill - he denied the robbery; I took Richards the same day.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270913-100]
1688. ABRAHAM LAZARUS was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of July, 3 half-crowns, 8 shillings, and 1 sixpence., the monies of Jeremiah Cockraine, from the person of Susannah Cockraine .
SUSANNAH COCKRAINE. I am the wife of Jeremiah Cockraine, he is a teacher of the classics. On the 26th of July, a little before nine o'clock in the morning, I went to Covent-garden-market with my little servant girl, who has now left me - I had a small basket in my hand, containing a green silk purse, with 16s. in it. I felt somebody press against me as I went through the market, which was much crowded. I saw the prisoner near me - I came out from the middle of the market, and felt the same pressure again, and then lost my purse - the prisoner was then close to me, and pressing me - it was safe five minutes before; my basket has two small lids; I saw one lid lifted up - I had it in front of me, and the prisoner was before me - I said,"I have lost my purse;" a gentleman took hold of him, and I took hold of his arm at the same time; he was secured instantly, and searched; some money was found on him, but not in my purse - my servant has left me. I saw a hand drawn out of my basket, but cannot say whose it was. I believe I have said it was the prisoners - my deposition was read over to me before I signed it, I think he is the man; I took hold of him, and accused him of it - he denied it; a sovereign, and 16s. 6d. were found on him.
Cross-examined by MR. J. ALLEY. Q. Might he not accidentally push against you? A. I do not know. I saw a hand come from my basket; the prisoner had not time to put the money out of the purse, for he was seized immediately. I did not swear to any of the money - I have my doubts now whether it was him.
WILLIAM DICKINSON. I am an officer. The prisoner was brought to the office; the prosecutrix positively charged him with the theft - I found a sovereign, fourteen shillings, and a had half-crown, all in his breeches pocket; it was restored him - there was two half-crowns.
MR. COCHRAINE. I think my money was three half crowns, a sixpence, and eight shillings.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270913-101]
1689. GEORGE WARD and JAMES EDWARDS were indicted for stealing, on the 24th of August, 1 watch, value 20s., the goods of James Whiteman.
JAMES WHITEMAN. I am a milkman, and live in Catherine-street, Lisson-grove. On the 24th of August, about eleven o'clock in the morning, I saw the prisoners together, within a yard of my house - I was a few yards off, talking to a person - I then missed Ward, and saw him come out of my shop - Edwards was sitting down on the step of my door - I am sure they were in company before; my shop door was open - my wife came out after Ward. and said to me, "Why don't you mind the shop, this man was in the parlour;" I then went in and missed the watch from the parlour, which was safe between nine and ten o'clock. I went in pursuit, and took Edwards about one hundred yards off, in two minutes; he had turned a corner; Ward was gone then; he was taken that evening.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you hear your wife say any thing to Ward? A. Yes; she asked what he did there; he said, "He came for a halfpenny worth of milk;" he spoke to Edwards, and they went away - no other person had been in the shop after I saw it safe - Ward had no business in the parlour.
Prisoner EDWARDS. Q. Did I make any resistance? A. No; I can swear to Ward.
SARAH WHITEMAN. I am the prosecutor's wife; I saw the watch last a little after ten o'clock; I left the shop about two minutes and went into the yard, with some dirty water; I came in again and saw Ward in the parlour; I said, "What do you want?" he said, "A halfpenny worth of milk;" he had a halfpenny in his hand; I said, We had none, and he went out - I walked out and told my husband he had been into the room; nobody else had been there since I had seen the watch - I saw Edwards sitting on the step, and heard Ward say to him, "They have no milk," and both went away together; I went in and missed the watch.
Cross-examined. Q. How far was Ward from where the watch was? A. About a yard or three-quarters; it is a small room; he walked out leasurely. I swear the watch was there at half-past ten o'clock - it did not go, but we had a clock in the room - I did not leave the room till I went into the yard.
THOMAS HOOKER. I am an officer. I apprehended Ward on the day of the robbery; he denied the charge - the watch has not been found.
Q. Did he not go of his own accord to the office? A Yes; I took him there.
EDWARDS' Defence. They swore before the Magistrate, they had not seen the watch after nine o'clock; then they altered it to ten and eleven - I came by and stopped to tie up my shoe; I went on; he came and saidhe wanted me, on suspicion of stealing the watch; no person had been with me.
WARD - GUILTY. Aged 18.
EDWARDS - GUILTY. Aged 23.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270913-102]
1690. ANN EASTON and HANNAH YOWBAND were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of August, 1 watch, value 8l.; 1 watch-chain, value 25s.; 2 seals, value 10s., and 1 key, value 4s., the goods of William Ashton, from his person.
WILLIAM ASHTON. I am a tailor, and live in St. Martin's-lane. On the 14th of August, about twelve o'clock at night. I was in King-street, Covent-garden, walking there, looking for a friend of mine, who I had lost - I was rather fresh - I had a watch in my fob, and felt it taken - I called out to the watchman; I believe it was a female who took it; I saw no man - I made a noise to the watchman, and told him I had lost it - I have found it since.
Q. Did you tell the Magistrate the prisoners had been talking to you? A. I did not; for I could not swear to them.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. You admit that you had drank a great deal? A. I had been with friends and drank took much. I could not have been long in the street.
Q. How many women had you been drinking with? A. I do not know - nor how many I had been talking to. I have some recollection of crying out of the watchman, and some women were about me. I lost a snuff-box also, and a handkerchief.
JOHN NICHOLLS. I am shopman to Messrs. Graham and Co., of Tothill-street. I have a seal and key pawned by Easton, in the name of Ann Wilson, about a quarter-past seven o'clock in the morning of the 15th of August.
JOHN ROBINSON JACKSON. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Tothill-street. I have a chain and seal pawned by Easton, on the 15th of August, between seven and eight o'clock in the morning.
ROBERT MARKS. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Tothill-street. I have a gold watch, pawned on the 15th of August, between one and two o'clock in the afternoon, by Easton to the best of my belief.
MICHAEL RAGAN. I am a watchman. On the 14th of August, I was on duty at the top of King-street, and saw Yowband and another woman running up a turning; the prosecutor came up and said "They have got my watch,""Which?" said I - "Those two women" said he. I ran and took Yowband in Bird-in-hand-court. I brought her back, and he said, "You have got my watch;" she said,"You know what I have got." I said, "If you have got it, give it him;" she then said she had not got it - he gave charge of her, and I took her to the watch-house, She then said she had given it to a woman named Smith, whom I went to take, but could not find. I cannot swear who was the other woman that was with her.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. I suppose the prosecutor was tolerably gone in liquor? A. Yes he was.
ANN HALL. My husband is a stone-mason. I did not know the prisoners till the morning of Tuesday, between nine and ten o'clock, when Easton came to my place with Kitty Smith - they came in and sat down. Smith took from Easton a handkerchief which was warpped up clumsily - she put the handkerchief into my hand, and said it was for me to keep till the middle of the day, I looked at Easton, and said, "There are two of you - I want to know what is in this." I gave it back, and they opened it; there was a watch in it, which looked like this one. They came again, and left a snuff-box and duplicate with me.
JOHN HICKS. I am a constable. About twelve o'clock at night, on the 14th of August, Yowband was brought into the watch-house; she said she had not the watch, but would tell me where to find it - if I went to No. 5, Viuegar-yard, and searched closely, I should find it, as Kitty Smith had got it. I went and found Smith's husband in bed, but she was not there; next morning, I went to Hall's, in Vine-street, and found this duplicate and a snuff-box.(Property produced and sworn to.)
EASTON'S Defence. I am perfectly innocent. I was not in Yowhand's company - I was in custody at the time the watch was pawned.
EASTON - GUILTY. Aged 25.
YOUBAND - GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18270913-103]
1691. JOHN FRAUD was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of August, 1 sovereign, 4 half-crowns, and 10 shillings, the monies of Henry Hawkins, from his person.
HENRY HAWKINS. I am a butcher. On the 31st of August, between nine and ten o'clock in the evening, I was rather in liquor - I had about 50s. about me in gold and silver; there were seven or eight shillings; the rest was a sovereign and half-crowns; the prisoner is a coachman - I hired his coach at the top of the Haymarket, and went to King-street - I then got on the box; my money was in my right hand breeches pocket, which was next to him; the coach went on to Bennett-street, Westminster, but it stopped several times before that - I first missed my money when I got off the box - I am positive it was safe when I got on - I then gave him in charge - I had felt him fumbling about me, and told him it would not do - I missed between 40s. and 50s.; he was searched, and two or three sixpences found on him - Sherriff was there part of the time.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Who was the friend you picked up? A. I had no one there; there was a lady there - I said nothing to her; the prisoner said he would take me to his house, to have a fowl dressed, which I had in my pocket - I had bought it in Oxford-street, about nine o'clock - I am not married - I will not swear I was sober - I drove the coach myself part of the way - I did not drive so long as a quarter of an hour; the lady got in near the Horse Guards, and got out in Bennett-street - I did not pay the prisoner - I will not swear that I did not say I had 5l. nor 1l. 12s. 6d.
Q. Had he any opportunity of going to put the money away? A. He went into a public-house; he came in there afterwards for his fare - I went into the public-house to give charge of him - I called the watchman directly, but he did not come directly - I will not say whether the watchman was drunk - I live at No. 7, Earl-street; he drove me to Villiers-street, as I went to see a friend, a butcher - I was in the coach when we were opposite Northumberland-house. there was a friend, of the prisoner's in the coach - I was robbed on the box - I cannot swear whether I was in the coach with his friend.
MARY ANN SHERRIFF. I was coming down Cockspur-street. The coachman accosted me; the butcher and the other man got out of the coach, and solicited me to get into it - I said I had no objection to a ride - I stepped in, and they all three got on the box together; they drove furiously on through several streets, and when I sat down, I saw the other man had got his hand in the prosecutor's pocket; he took out his money; he and the coachman shared the money between them, behind the prosecutor's back; when I got down I saw the traces of the coach were broken, in consequence of their furious driving.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? I work at the braid trade - I did not know the prosecutor before; it was folly in me to get into the coach: no one was inside with me. I heard the butcher say he had got a fowl in his pocket - I had been taking my work home - I did not hear him say he was robbed of 5l.; he said it was between 2l. and 3l. I live in Bennett-street - I was married fourteen years ago. I dare say I told them I lived in Bennett-street - I do not know whether I did not; the coachman took me imprudently round the waist, and then the other man asked me to go in, and I did; the coachman was tipsy. I do not think he knew what he was about.
COURT. Q. Who drove the coach? A. The butcher first, and then the other man; they drove furiously, and tried to upset the coach - I was in the coach when I saw the other man take the money; it was a charriot - I put out my head, and said, I saw what they had done - the man put out his fist, and said he would put my two eyes into one, and knock me out of the window.
JOSEPH SALT. I am a japanner, and live in Horseferry-road. I was going down Princes-street, Westminster, and saw the coach stop - I saw a young man get off the box, and run away; the coachman said, "Cut, you b - g - r; cut as quick as possible!" the coachman was at that time off the box; the butcher found he was robbed - I said,"Take his number, and you will find him in the morning;" we went into a public-house; the coachman came in about three quarters of an hour, with two other men; he made a noise, and said, he would punch our heads about if we said he had robbed him; he then pulled out his money, which was only two or three sixpences; but he had been absent three quarters of an hour; the coach was No. 19.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the coachman so drunk as not to know what he was doing? A. He was not sober, but he knew what he was doing; he was drunk.
Mr. PHILLIPS called -
JOHN LITHGO. I am a watchman, and live in Perkins'-rents, Westminster. I remember seeing the prosecutor on the night in question; he said at first that he had been robbed of 5l. I did not hear him say it was 2l. 10s. - the first word he said was, he had been robbed of 2l. I was calling the hour, and then went up to the coachman, and said he was my prisoner; the prosecutor then said, "You scoundrel, you have robbed me of 5l.; take charge of him." I said I could not take him from his horses; the prosecutor then said he was robbed of 35s.; he was in liquor, but knew what he was doing. I looked at the coach minutely; no trace was broken - I know Sherriff to be a common prostitute in Bennett-street; this is my beat - I have seen her there with men, like other prostitutes.
MARY ANN SHERRIFF re-examined. I have lived three years at Mrs. Smith's, No. 10. Little George-street, Hampstead-road, and was there till last Saturday fortnight. I have lived at No. 4, Bennett-street, only for the last three weeks, and have never been out after ten o'clock at night - I am not in the habit of getting into coaches - I thought no harm of going in this coach. I had been carrying home a large bundle - I never got my living as a prostitute, or picked up men in the street. I am to return to Mrs. Smith's next Saturday, or to No. 10, Bowling-green-lane.
JOSEPH SALT. I have been to this woman's house, and always found her hard at work at her needle.
GUILTY. Aged 46.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270913-104]

The prisoner pleaded GUILTY. Aged 51.

1693. ELIZA EDWARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of September, 1 handkerchief, value 6d.; 1 key, value 2d.; 1 tobacco-stopper, value 6d., and 3 shillings, the property of John Bex, from his person.
JOHN BEX. I am a collar-maker. On the 1st of September, between nine and eleven o'clock in the day time, I met the prisoner and another woman in the street; we went to a public-house, and had some liquor and some coffee. I was not quite sober; I laid my head on the table, and went to sleep. I awoke in about an hour, and the prisoner was then gone; I missed my handkerchief out of my hat; also a key, a tobacco-stopper, and 3s., from my pocket. I saw the prisoner again on the 3d of September, in Tottenham-court-road, with the handkerchief round her neck; it has not my name on it, but I knew it by this mark, which had been made in my presence.
Prisoner. I met this man with another woman; we went to a public-house at the corner of Charles-street, and had five half-pints of gin between three of us. I was perfectly sober, but the other woman was very sick, and she went out; he lent her that blue handkerchief to wipe her face with - we then went to another house, and when I got out I found the handkerchief in my hand, but I was in liquor before I parted with him - he went to the bar to change a sovereign, but the landlady said he had got silver in his hand.
THOMAS SIMONS. I am a watchman. I took up the prisoner on the 3d of September, and found this handkerchief about her neck.
JOHN BEX. I did go to change a sovereign, and the landlady could not give me change; she said she had seen some silver in my hand - there had been no one in the room but us two. I had my handkerchief in my hat, by my side, on the table.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270913-106]
1694. JOHN OSBORN was indicted for stealing, onthe 13th of July, 1 necklace, value 5s., the goods of Edward Palmer, the elder, from the person of Edward Palmer, the younger .
MARY PALMER. I am the wife of Edward Palmer; we live at No. 6, Long's-court, Leicester-square. On the 13th of July I was out with my son, Edward, in my arms. I felt the child go back in my arms - I turned round and saw the prisoner with the necklace in his hand - he was putting it into his trousers; he ran off - I gave an alarm, and followed - a gentleman stopped him, but he had not then got it; he had turned a corner. I am quite certain he had had it.
Prisoner's Defence. I had not seen it.

[trialAccount t18270913-107]
1695. HENRY BARRELL was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of July, 1 fixture, that is to say, 1 iron stove, value 10s., the goods of John Serancke, and fixed to a building belonging to him, against the statute.
ISABELLA HEFFER. Mr. Serancke left the key of the house, No. 5, Euston-crescent, with my sister, who was to show it. On the 17th of July the prisoner came and asked to look at it. I went and showed it him - he was very particular in going round, and said he would call in the morning upon Mr. Serancke; he went away, and said no more; when he left I pulled the door to and locked it; this was about eight o'clock in the morning - the parlour stove was then safe, and the copper in the back kitchen. I locked the parlour door, but left the key on the outside.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you know any thing of this man? A. No, but I am sure he is the man.
LUCY BAKER. I live at No. 4, Euston-crescent. My little boy told me the next morning, between nine and ten o'clock, that a man had gone out with this stove - I looked out, and saw the prisoner with it. I gave no alarm, as I thought it might be quite right. I afterwards heard the door shut again - I looked up, and saw the prisoner with some covings on his shoulder - I then sent to the person who had the key, to know if any person had fetched the key away; Miss Heffer came, and said she could not open the door, there must be some one inside. I went to the door, and sent my boy for a constable; and while he was gone the prisoner came out with this other stove.
Cross-examined. Q. Then he was taken? A. Yes, he passed our house, and was taken.
JOHN HEFFER. I was set to watch the premises by Mrs. Baker. I saw the prisoner open the door with a latch-key - I saw him come out with a stove on his shoulder - I followed him up Curzon-street, and he was stopped with it.
CHARLES JOHNSON. I am a constable. I was sent for, and in going towards the house I met the prisoner with this stove. I stopped him - in going back, he said he was bringing it from his own house in Seymour-street; when we got to Euston-crescent we met the boy, who said he was the man who brought it out of the house - the prisoner then said he was employed by Mr. Sabine - I took him there, and heard from Mr. Sabine's clerk that he had no authority to go there; he ran from me, but I caught him again - I found some keys on him, one of which fits the latch of the door, and one fits the parlour door.
CHARLES SABINE. I am an attorney, and agent to Mr. John Serancke. This house was in my care, and I had the letting of it for him - I know the prisoner, but had not employed him. I had been over the house a few days before.
Cross-examined. Q. You say you knew him? A. Yes, I had employed him to repair the house about two years ago.
GEORGE DICKINSON. I am clerk to Mr. Sabine. I went to the house and missed a stove from the parlour, and one from the front room first floor.
Prisoner. I wish to throw myself on the mercy of the Court; my children were in want of bread. I was formerly in business, but have been greatly reduced.
GUILTY. Aged 31.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18270913-108]
1696. THOMAS RICE was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of September, 1 watch, value 3l., and 1 watch-key, value 6d., the goods of Joseph Copping, from his person.
JOSEPH COPPING. On the 1st of September, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening, I was going along Whitechapel, when the prisoner came and snatched my watch and seal from my fob; he ran off - I followed, and never lost sight of him till I took him with them in his hand.
THOMAS YEARLEY. I heard the cry of Stop thief! and saw the prisoner running across the road - he made his way towards Goulston-street - he was brought back, and I took hold of him; Copping came up, and said, "That is the man that took my watch;" the prisoner had no watch then, but Copping said he had taken the watch from him before - that he got from him again, and I took him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I heard the cry of Stop thief! and this man came and took me - Copping said I was the man, but I never saw the watch.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18270913-109]
1697. MARTHA SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of August, 1 watch, value 30s.; 1 watch-chain, value 7s.; 1 seal, value 5s.; 1 watch-key, value 2s.; 1 half-sovereign, and 2l. 5s., in monies numbered, the property of Samson Maynard, from his person.
SAMSON MAYNARD. I am a cow-keeper. I was at a public-house in Fleet-market; I left my horse and cart at the door, and went into the house; the prisoner came in while I was there - I had seen her twice before: we drank together. She then got up into the cart with me, and she drove the cart away - she took me up Holborn-hill, to the Wheatsheaf, public-house, and gave me something to drink- she then took me to a bad house; I recollect going up stairs, and sitting on a bed; I was very sick, and she took hold of my head, and said, "My dear, I will hold your head;" she then got her hand into my pocket - I said,"Don't rob me;" she said No, but she took out my money and my watch, by force. I then got on the bed, and the lady of the house came up, and called the patrol; she said she should be searched. I found some of my money on the drawers in the morning, but I lost 45s.
WILLIAM RICHARDSON. I am a patrol of St. Ann's parish. I went to the house between seven and eight o'clock, on the 20th of August - I found the prosecutor very tipsy: I asked the prisoner if she had any property to give it to me - she would not, because she said she did not know whatI was. I then searched her, and took from her a watch and some money - I had been on duty before, and saw her and the prosecutor going along in the cart: I followed them, and told the woman of the house that the cart must go to the Green-yard, if the man did not come and take it away.
SARAH CURTIS. I live at No. 2, Pheasant-buildings, Gray's Inn-lane. The prisoner and the prosecutor came there, and had a room in my mistress' house; the prisoner called me, and said her friend was very sick and ill, and asked me to get a little brandy for him, and she gave me half-a-crown; my mistress then said she should not go away without the prosecutor, and sent me for the patrol, and insisted on her being searched. The watch was found in her possession - she said she was going home to his wife.
HANNAH CODD. I keep this house. The prisoner was going down - I said she should not go without the man; I went and shook him, and asked if he had a watch about him - he was not sensible, but he said at last he had bought a watch for 2l. that day.
JOHN BLAKE. I took the prisoner the next morning; I found a bit of a broken watch-glass on her.(Watch produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. When the patrol came up stairs I would not give it up, because I did not know what authority he had; I said I was as well able to keep it as he was - he then took it from me, and the prosecutor told him to call in the morning.
Prisoner's Defence (written). I met him at this public-house, and, knowing him, went in the cart with him; when we got to Gray's Inn-lane, he had more drink, and made me almost tipsy; he gave me the money to take care of, and take to his wife; a man said the cart would be taken to the Green-yard, and I went in search of the cart; he, missing me, asked for his property, and the landlady gave me in custody.
GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18270913-110]
1698. WILLIAM ANDERSON was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of August, 6 yards of printed cotton, value 13s., and 1 shawl, value 25s., the goods of James Lynch.
CATHERINE LYNCH. I am the wife of James Lynch, a porter - we live in Oxford-buildings, Oxford-street. The prisoner lodged in my house on the 11th of August - he slept there on the Friday night, and on the Saturday night I left him to mind my room while I went out to buy a cap; when I returned in a quarter of an hour my little child, whom I had left at home, was crying - the prisoner had broken a lock, and taken my shawl, which he had in his hand - I said, "What are you doing?" he said nothing. I laid hold of the shawl, but he got away, and I could not hold him; my neighbours were in bed - my husband came home soon after: he went after the prisoner all night, but could not find him; we then offered a reward. I afterwards missed the cotton - I have never seen the property since.
Prisoner. Q. Can you stand before the Judge, and say you saw me with the shawl? A. Yes, and you said you would knock me down if I stopped you.
JAMES LYNCH. I am the husband of the last witness. - I came home about ten o'clock, and heard what had happened; the prisoner and the property were then gone - he was taken on the 19th of August, but nothing was found on him: he was in a lodging-house, with his arm round a girl's neck; he said to me, "I don't know you;" I said,"Don't you know where you lodged?" he said, "Oh! I know you now."
ROBERT TURNER. I took the prisoner - he denied all knowledge of the property.
Prisoner's Defence. I did lodge in the house one night. I heard an uproar in the street, and went out to see; I did not come back that night, that is the truth - they keep a very bad bawdy-house.
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18270913-111]
1699. JOHN BRYAN was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of June, 5 putlogs, value 2s., the goods of James Hart; and 1 piece of board, value 2d., the goods of Samuel Lewis Hart.
JAMES HART. I am the sub-contractor at the London Docks; one of these putlogs are mine, and the others I had borrowed - the prisoner had worked there, but had left, being unwell - I went to his house with the officer - I called out at the door, "Bryan, have you any thing of mine?" a voice within said, "Nothing;" things were sometimes left at his house, which was the reason of my asking - I went behind the house, and saw this putlog thrown out of a window, and the officer produced another.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was the prisoner in bed? A. No; he was unwell - he had the care of several of these things - I desired one Riley to take some rammers to his house, but no putlogs - I think it was done by somebody else; I should be proud to employ him again.
JOHN STEPTOE. I am a bricklayer. I was at work at the docks, and missed a putlog, which I think I had seen on the scaffold the day before.
JAMES JEFFRIES. I am a constable. I found this putlog under his bed; he bore a very good character.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270913-112]
1700. JOHN BARNES was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of July, 2 sovereigns, and 1 sixpence, the monies of Zackariah Brewster.
MARY ANN BREWSTER. I am the wife of Zackariah Brewster, a warehouseman - we live in the Curtain-road, where I keep a milliner's shop. On the 28th of July, about eleven o'clock, I saw my two sovereigns and a sixpence safe in a piece of paper, in the till - I was in the back parlour at half-past twelve o'clock, and heard the lock of the till go - I turned and saw the prisoner with his arms across the counter - I asked what he wanted; he said, "A ready made shirt;" I said, "I keep no such things;" he went out - I looked at the till and missed the money - I ran out caught him, and asked him for the money - he said, he knew nothing about it; I have not found it.
JOSEPH BIRCH. I am an officer. I was sent for, and took the prisoner - he said he went in to buy a shirt - he had no money about him, and I said he had no money to pay for it; he said if there had been one to suit him, he should call for it in the evening.
Prisoner's Defence. I went in and knocked on the counter; she said she did not sell shirts - she came out and took me, about two yards off - she went back and took out six or seven sovereigns - she said, "These are not what I want;" there were two in a paper.
MRS. BREWSTER. There were four other sovereigns in another part of the till - I had not been out of the shop a quarter of an hour.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270913-113]
1701. ELIZABETH BOYLE was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of August, 1 pelisse, value 20s., the goods of Edward Baylis.
GEORGE MELLISH GRAHAM. I am servant to Edward Baylis, pawnbroker, of Hampstead-road. The prisoner came in to pawn an article on the 7th of August, but refused to take what was offered her; I was folding up some goods, and had a pelisse laying there - I was called away for a moment, and on returning, she was gone and the pelisse also; I sent a lad after her, but he could not find her; she came again in the afternoon, with the article, and said she would take the money we had offered her: I said we had lost a pelisse: she said if I thought she had it, I had better take her; in the evening Stack brought the pelisse to pawn, and said where she got it: I went with her, and found the prisoner in bed - she was taken.
MARGARET STACK. The prisoner gave me the pelisse to pawn.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence (written.) I went to the shop to pawn a coat - I was in liquor, and when I got home with it, I found this pelisse in it, which I was ignorant of.
GEORGE M. GRAHAM. She did not appear in liquor - she frequented our shop; she said it had been folded up with the coat.
GUILTY. Aged 40.
Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18270913-114]
1702. DANIEL CORCORAN was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of August, 1 shawl, value 1s., and 1 pair of boots, value 5s., the goods of Levy Hart.
LEVY HART. I am a salesman, and live in Monmouth-street. On the 9th of August, about nine o'clock at night, the prisoner, three other men, and a woman, came into my shop, and looked at some hats; while I was looking for one, they pushed down a horse with clothes on it, and while I was picking it up, the prisoner went out with the shawl; I followed, and collared him three yards off, and took it from him - the boots were wrapped up in it - he had taken them off the counter.
TIMOTHY RYAN. I took him in charge.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I went with two soldiers to buy a hat; one of them was tipsy, and went against the horse, which fell down; I declare I never saw the things till I got to the office.
GUILTY. Aged 51.
Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18270913-115]
1703. JOHN EVANS was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of July, 1 ham, value 3s., the goods of Charles Docker.
HENRY COOKE. I am shopman to Charles Docker, cheesemonger, of Great Chapel-street, Westminster. I was at tea on the 20th of July; Norris came in, and said a man had got a ham; I went and saw the prisoner at the counter, with something in his apron; I asked what he had there: he said, nothing: I put my hand under his apron, and this ham fell on the floor.
JOHN NORRIS. I am shopman to Mr. Docker; the prisoner came in, and asked for a rasher of bacon, and while I turned he took the ham.
JOHN SIMPSON. I was sent for, and took him; he had no money whatever.
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that he was intoxicated, and did not know that he had the ham, and pleading distress.
GUILTY. Aged 38.
Recommended to Mercy. - Fined 1s. and Discharged.

EDWARD THOMAS ROGERS. These fowls are not mine, but my brother's.
NOT GUILTY.

1705. RICHARD HOWARD was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of August, 1 cheese, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Henry Bonsor.
HENRY BONSOR. I am a cheesemonger, and live at Shadwell. I was sitting in my back room, and saw three men pass my window twice, and look in: I heard a noise the second time, like something being drawn off the board; I ran out and missed the cheese; I went into the street, and saw the prisoner running down the turning with something under his arm; I called Stop thief! and he dropped the cheese - he was secured, without my losing sight of him.
ROBERT JEFFREY. I received him in charge.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 20.

[trialAccount t18270913-118]
1706. GEORGE HARRIS was indicted for stealing on the 7th of August, 1 handkerchief, value 5s., the goods of John Manley Gerrish.
ELIZA GERRISH. I am the wife of John Manley Gerrish. On the 7th of August I had this handkerchief to wash for a gentleman; I missed it about two o'clock in the afternoon, from the end of a table in my room: I had seen it a few minutes before - the prisoner had brought some bread to my house that day; I went up stairs, and when I returned he stood with his back to the table.
JOHN MANLEY GERRISH. I went next morning with an officer to a house, and found the prisoner in bed with a young woman; I told him about the handkerchief; he asked if I had any children to take it away; I told him No, and he had better give it up, as my wife would lose the family washing: I then gave charge of him, and, as we went along, he said it was pawned, and he had thrown away the duplicate, but the officer found it on the witness.
EDMUND GROVER. On the morning the prisoner of him; I taken, I bought the duplicate of a handkerchief of him; I sent my wife to the pawnbroker's, and she brought back this handkerchief; I put it on my neck; the officer and Mr. Gerrish came and claimed it.
RICHARD CONSTANTINE. I am an officer, and apprehended the prisoner; he at first said he had not taken it; we then went to a public-house; some people there said to him, "Why don't you tell the truth? the poor woman will lose her washing:" he then took me to the pawnbroker, who said he was the man who pawned it; he then told me who he had sold the duplicate to.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270913-119]
1707. THOMAS JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of August, 1 saddle, value 10s.; 1 bridle, value 5s.; 1 pair of overalls, value 10s., and 1 pair of kneecaps, value 5s., the goods of George Johnson.
GEORGE JOHNSON. I live at the turnpike office, Bromley; I found my shed broken open, and missed this property - I know nothing of the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOSEPH SEARCH. I am a watchman. On the 30th of August, between two and three o'clock in the morning, I went to Mr. Johnson's stable, to look for some bad characters, and found the prisoner sitting in the shed; he had the stirrups, leather, and bridle in his pocket; the other things were packed up, near to where he was sitting: he said he came there to sleep - the harness room was broken open.
THOMAS BARFORD. I am a constable, and received the prisoner in charge. I found on him a screw-driver, which I compared with some marks on the harness room door - it fits them exactly.
JOSEPH LEWELL. I am in the employ of Mr. George Johnson; I locked all this property up safe at night.
GUILTY. Aged 70.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18270913-120]
1708. MARY ANN KENNEY was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of July, 7 yards of printed cotton, value 7s., the goods of Robert Robertson; and a veil, value 10s., the goods of Caroline Jane Monk.
CAROLINE JANE MONK. I am chambermaid to Robert Robertson, of Oxendon-street. On the 17th of July, the prisoner, who had been my housemaid, left the service, and about an hour after she was gone, I missed fifteen sovereigns from my box; I went with an officer to her lodgings; a white veil of mine was found in her bonnet-box next day - the cotton was found in her room; I had missed the veil three weeks before.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What kind of a house is this? A. A house of accommodation; I believe it is called a brothel; master does not make presents to the servants; a glass was once missed, and it was found under the bed.
JAMES COLLINS. I am an upholsterer; I was at work at this house, and went in search of the prisoner - I went to her lodgings with the officer, and broke her box open.
JOSEPH MEDCALF. I am an officer. I went to her room, and found this curtain in a box; I afterwards found the veil in the bonnet-box, which she said was her sister's.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not her sister taken up? A. Yes, but she was discharged.
COURT. Q. Where was the bonnet-box? A. That was pointed out to me by another person next day - it was in the room the night before, but I did not open it; she said both boxes were her sister's at first.
JAMES COLLINS. I can swear to this cotton - it was missing from a bed which I was putting up there - we sent to the callenderer's for it, thinking it had not come home.
CAROLINE JANE MONK re-examined. I do not hire the servants - I manage the house; the prisoner left without giving warning - she had been five or six weeks with us.
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Of stealing the cotton only.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18270913-121]
1709. JOHN LANCASTER was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of July, 1 jacket, value 2s.; 1 waistcoat, value 1s.; 1 handkerchief, value 2d., and 1 cap, value 2s., the goods of Thomas Barker.
THOMAS BARKER. I am servant to Mr. Elliott, of Finsbury-pavement. On the 29th of July I put my things into the coach-house about half-past ten o'clock, and missed them in about an hour.
BENJAMIN BISHOP. I lived at the same place with Barker. I saw the prisoner go into the coach-house, and come out with these things - the cap was on his head; he saw me, and threw them into a gig in the yard; we wanted him to go about his business, but he would not. I took the cap from him, and gave him in charge, because he would not go away. I saw him take the jacket from his hat.
Prisoner's Defence. I went down the yard to the privy - they perceived me; they took my hat off, and I would not go away without it - they did not want me but my hat.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270913-122]
1710. JAMES RICHARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of August, 1 pair of breeches, value 20s.; 9 waistcoats, value 12s.; 8 handkerchiefs, value 6s.; 10 pairs of stockings, value 8s.; 2 pencil-cases, value 2s., and 3 caps, value 2s., the goods of George Crowfoot.
GEORGE CROWFOOT. I am a groom. I lodged at the Dolphin public-house, in Oxford-street - I had been into the country about seven weeks. I left my box locked, and took the key with me; when I returned I found it still locked, but nothing in it; the prisoner lodged at the house - five pairs of stockings, and a night cap were found on him.
GEORGE BALL. I keep the Dolphin. I had the prisoner secured, and asked how he came to take the things away; he said he had taken nothing but the stockings, and they were found in his room - he said at the office that he had worn them, but he did not say so then.
ELIZABETH REX. I am a laundress. On the 15th of August the prisoner brought me two shirts, four handkerchiefs, and six pairs of stockings to wash - Crowfoot claimed three pairs of the stockings. I delivered them to Mrs. Ball.
WILLIAM KIMBERLEY. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and found a pair of stockings in his hat - he said he had worn them - some others were found in his room.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. Twelve or fourteen people lodged in the house. I thought these stockings were my own - they were laying about.
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Transported for Seven Years.

1711. JOHN TOMLINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of July, 30 penny-pieces, and 60 halfpence, the monies of Charles Richardson.
CHARLES RICHARDSON. I am a cheesemonger, and live in Whitecross-street. On the 14th of July, between eleven and twelve o'clock at night, the prisoner came into my shop, and tasted some bacon and cheese, but bought none - another person came in, which took my attention;the prisoner went down the shop, round the counter, went up to my desk, on which was 5s. in copper - he went out. I went after him, and as I took hold of him, he dropped the paper of copper - I find he bears a most excellent character.
GEORGE GARRETT. I am a watchman. I saw Richardson stop the prisoner, and the paper of copper fell from him.
CHARLES GAMBLE. The prisoner was brought to the watch-house - I found 9d. on him.
The prisoner stated that he had committed the offence under the influence of intoxication.
GUILTY. Aged 34.

[trialAccount t18270913-124]
1712. ANN MOSELEY was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of July, 2 sheets, value 6s.; 1 frock, value 2s.; 2 blankets, value 2s.; 1 cloth, value 1s.; 4 napkins, value 2s.; 2 pockets, value 6d.; 2 pillow-cases, value 1s.; 2 remnants of lace, value 2s.; 1 apron, value 6d.; 1 bottle, value 1s.; 1 pair of gloves, value 3d., and 1 cap, value 3d., the goods of Daniel Gardiner; and MARY SPAREY was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, knowing them to have been stolen, against the statute.
DANIEL GARDINER. I am a brush-maker, and live in Spitalfields - Mosely was nurse in my family, from Easter to July, when she left to go to see her friends - I missed the table-cloth from my bed-room drawer the night she left - a dirty one had been put in its place; next day I found the drawers were stripped of my wife's wearing apparel - the articles stated were gone among others. I got a search-warrant; we found Mosely at work, at a house in Rosemary-lane, where she had gone from my house - the officer searched her, and found some duplicates on her - we went in a coach to her lodgings; we found Sparey sitting there; she said something to her - the officer found a quantity of things there; they have my wife's name on them; my wife died a few days after this, it gave her such a turn when she heard of it - she had been ill for some time. Sparey had called to see Moseley when she was at my house.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Did not you at first find Sparey in the room, and make her tell you where Moseley was? A. Yes; the officer said he had a search-warrant; they told us where most of the things were. I have been robbed by my nephew.
THOMAS VANN. I am an officer, and took Moseley - as we were in the coach, I saw her put her hand into her pocket; I said, "I must search first." I found some duplicates on her - she then begged Mr. Gardiner's pardon; we found Sparey at the lodgings. I said I had a warrant, and they had better give up the things, which she did - Sparey said, "Moseley had brought them, and told her the sheets would do to shift her bed;" she seemed near her confinement - Moseley said Sparey knew nothing of it.
EDWARD RUBY. I am a pawnbroker, of Whitechapel: I have a sheet, shirt, and handkerchief, pawned by Moseley for 7s., and a tea-spoon, for 1s. 6d., in the name of Mary Gorby.
JOHN YOWART. I am a pawnbroker, and have a tablecloth, pawned by Moseley.
MOSELEY's Defence. The things I took to Sparey's I had bought for her. I had pawned the other articles for Mrs. Gardiner, who was a very drunken woman, and lived on brandy, rum and gin, all the time I was there.
DANIEL GARDINER. My wife was deranged for twelve months; the prisoner had the care of her.
MOSELEY - GUILTY. Aged 57.
Confined One Month.
SPAREY - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270913-125]
1713. WILLIAM TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of July, 1 rug, value 7s., the goods of William Cowen.
SOPHIA COWEN. I am the wife of William Cowen, a broker - we live in Clerkenwell; this rug was stolen from a chair in our shop, about half-past eleven o'clock in the morning.
GEORGE WADDINGTON. I was in Compton-street; the prisoner passed me with the rug: I followed him, and he dropped it - I laid hold of him; he said it was his first offence, and if I would forgive him, he would not do so again; I did not lose sight of him.
Prisoner's Defence. I picked it up.
GUILTY. Aged 13.

1714. ESTHER BRANSOM was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of August, 1 shawl, value 4s.; 1 clock, value 6s., and 1 frock, value 2s., the goods of Abraham Cooper, her master.
MARIA COOPER. I am the wife of Abraham Cooper; the prisoner was in our service on the 9th of August, and on the 10th I gave her in charge; she said nothing to me - she said she had lent the articles to her brother, to keep him dry, as it rained hard, but that was not stated to me - she was a year and eight months with us.
WALTER LEY. I am an officer. I produce a cloak and shawl, which I got from the prisoner's brother - I found the prisoner in the watch-house; she said she lent the cloak and shawl to her brother, and he had pawned the other articles, which she had given him.
JOHN BRANSOM. I am the prisoner's brother; she gave me the shawl and cloak - I had been out of work, and asked her for 4s. - she said she had no money, but would give me a cloak and whittle of her own, which I pawned for 4s.
WILLIAM ROBERTS. I am a pawnbroker, and have a frock and some patchwork, pawned in the name of Bransom - I do not know who by.
WILLIAM CLUNES. I am a pawnbroker; the prisoner's brother pawned this cloak and shawl with me - they were redeemed again before I heard of this.
JOHN HUTT. I took the prisoner into custody; she said she lent her brother the cloak and shawl one wet night; her brother brought them to the watch-house.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I lent him them for a week.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Strongly recommended to Mercy. - Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18270913-127]
1715. FRANCES CARTWRIGHT was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of July, 12 pairs of socks, value 1s. 6d., and 24 yards of calico, value 11s., the goods of William Jones, her master.
WILLIAM JONES. I am a linen-draper and live in Tottenham-court-road. The prisoner was in my service about six weeks; I missed this property from my warehouse - I received information and found them in her box; which the housekeeper pointed out; it was not locked.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did she not deny all knowledge of the socks? A. At first she did; I sent for the officer, she then seemed to show great contrition; and said she hoped I would forgive her.
GEORGE AVIS. I am an officer, and took her.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18270913-128]
1716. OSBORN PANTON was indicted for embezzlement.
ROBERT STONE. I am a job-master and live in High-street, Mary-le-bone; the prisoner was my clerk at a wharf which I have at Milbank-street, Westminster; it was his busines to take orders and receive money, which he was to account for to Mr. Jeffries daily.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long had he lived with you? A. Twelve months; I am a coal and corn dealer at that wharf.
THOMAS MARTIN. I live in Osnaburg-street, and deal with Mr. Stone. On the 12th of April I paid the prisoner a cheque for 31l. 6s., on Sir Claude Scott, on Stone's account - here is his receipt for it.
JOHN LLOYD. I am clerk at Sir Claude Scotts. On the 12th of April, I cashed this cheque on account of Mr. Martin - I gave five 5l notes, six sovereigns, and the rest in silver.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know you paid it? A. By a mark on the cheque, and the entry in the book.
JOSEPH JEFFRIES. I am in the employ of Mr. Stone - the prisoner used to bring me the cash, just as he received it - he never accounted to me for any money received from Mr. Martin; he paid me nothing that day - and never accounted for this.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. I conduct Mr. Stone's business, as a friend; I receive no salary - I have no part of the profits - it was his duty to bring me the cheque or the money.
COURT. Q. There would have been nothing said if he got it cashed, and brought you the money? A. No.
GUILTY. Aged 26.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
There was another indictment against the prisoner.
[trialAccount t18270913-129]
1717. WILLIAM SMART was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of August, 2 deal boards, value 9s., the goods of Samuel Beaumont Cheeswright, his master.
SAMUEL BEAUMONT CHEESWRIGHT. I am a timber merchant, and live in Kingsland-road - the prisoner was my sawyer, and had no authority to take boards from my yard.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you authorize me to take out a plank that day? A. No; I left word for the carman to take it - but these are deals; the prisoner absconded next day - there was 5s. or 6s. due to him; when he was taken he said he took the deals out of the canal, but they were dry.
EBENEZA CHEESWRIGHT. On the 1st of August I saw the prisoner take a deal out of my father's yard, about eleven o'clock in the morning; and in the evening I saw him down the yard, tipsy, and in a few minutes he went down to the canal - I saw him afterwards with another deal on his shoulder; he had taken it through a place in the pales - I ran and told him to bring it back; he said he would not - for Mr. Barlow had told him to take it.
MARY DOLTON. I am servant to the prosecutor and saw the prisoner take a six feet deal, out about eleven o'clock in the morning; and in the evening I saw him crossing the bridge with another.
HENRY RAY. I am carman to the prosecutor; his son called me in the evening to go after the prisoner - but he was gone.
GEORGE SMITH. I am an officer. I went to the prosecutor's yard, and found an opening, through which a deal appeared to have been drawn.
Prisoner's Defence. I took none but what I was sent with by Mr. Cheeswright.
GUILTY. Aged 34.
Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18270913-130]
1718. JOHN BURY was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of September, 2lbs. of yarn, value 3s., the goods of Henry Keat and Ebeneza Keat.
EBENEZA KEAT. I am in partnership with Henry Keat, my father; we are rug manufacturers. On the 5th of September, between six and seven o'clock in the morning, I was called up - and waited outside, till the prisoner came out of the premises - I took his hat off, and found this yarn in it; he had once worked for us.
JOHN NASH. I am in Mrssrs. Keats' employ; the prisoner came there, and I saw him take the yarn - I gave information.

1719. JAMES BENNETT was indicted for stealing, on the 8th July, I live tame fowl, price 18d., and 1 dead fowl, value 18d., the property of Thomas Perry.
THOMAS PERRY. I keep the Water Works, public-house, Belgrave-place. On the 8th of August, at six o'clock in the morning, I fed my fowls, and they were all right - in the afternoon I missed one. I went on the wharf and saw a quantity of feathers; I had missed another a few days before.
JOHN SOUTHERWOOD. I am an officer. I found this fowl at the prisoner's house, in a small lane, near Chelseacommon - and I found him on the wharf, with a dead fowl in a bag, which I saw him take up; it was quite warm - he said he found it.
THOMAS PERRY. The dead fowl is the one I lost that day - I missed the live one some days before.
Prisoner's Defence. I picked up the dead one on the wharf; I did not know the live one was at my house.
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18270913-132]
1720. JAMES BOTTOMY was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of August, 1 till, value 1s.; 1 shilling, 12 sixpences, and the sum of 6s. 31/2d. in copper monies, the property of James Ayres.
JAMES AYRES. I keep a baker's shop in Goldsmith's-row, Hackney-road. On the 15th of August I was in thebake-house - my mother called me, and said the till was gone - I ran out, and found it in the middle of the field, about a quarter of a mile from my house. I picked up some money by it.
MARY BYERS. Between one and two o'clock, I was in my son's passage, and saw the prisoner in the shop, reaching over the counter - I asked what he wanted, he turned round and ran out. I saw him running very fast - I did not see the till in his hand - I am quite certain of his person - Mrs. Smith came up.
SUSANNAH SARAH SMITH. I saw the prisoner come out of the shop with the till in his hand - he ran very fast towards the field - I ran out and followed him. I saw him throw the till down in the middle of the field - he then ran very fast, but I saw him caught, and am certain he is the man - I lost sight of him for a moment.
JOHN MARTIN. I took the prisoner in the middle of Clement's field - I did not see the till.
THOMAS FRANKLIN. I am an officer, and took him in custody.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going home, and saw several people running - I ran, and was taken.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270913-133]
1721. WILLIAM BAKER was indicted for stealing, on the 23d of July, 1 pair of boots, value 4s., the goods of Walker Brewster.
WALKER BREWSTER. I am a perfumer. On the 23d of July I missed these boots from my kitchen - the prisoner was in my employ, and had to clean them - he said he knew nothing of them.
JAMES WALLIS. I am a pawnbroker, and have a pair of boots pawned in the name of John Baker - I cannot say who by.
JOSEPH COLLINS. I am a constable. I took the prisoner - he said he had pawned the boots, that the duplicate was at his mother's lodgings, in a box, and he meant to redeem them. GUILTY. Aged 15.
